# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-11-03

| **[RoboArchitect: Automating the Design of Robotic Agents with Persistent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42141v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent work shows that a frozen robot policy performs far better inside an agent harness, where control code decides when to query the policy, consult vision tools, or command the arm directly. Yet today these harnesses are built by hand and redesigned for each new robot or benchmark. We show that harness design can be automated, offering a strong alternative to finetuning on a novel setup. In RoboArchitect, a coding model observes a robot's rollouts, diagnoses failures, and iteratively revises the harness around the frozen policy. The harness evolves on a set of episodes for its optimization and is evaluated on held-out scenes and tasks. Without updating any model weights, RoboArchitect raises average success from 9.7% to 43.3% on unseen scenes (4.5x) and from 12.7% to 40.2% on unseen tasks (3.2x), across multiple benchmarks and policies. The gains are largest where the benchmark is far from the policy's training data: wrapping a general-purpose policy, the evolved harness yields a specialist that often outperforms even the finetuned version. Intuitive control strategies are automatically designed, such as lifting a jammed arm clear or reaching in horizontally when an obstacle blocks the way from above. The harness also evolves its own persistent memory, carrying learned experience, such as effective grasp depths, into later decisions. These results suggest that substantial headroom in robotic manipulation lies in the agent harness, and its design can be effectively automated and optimized without weight updates.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Website: https://roboarchitect-ai.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[Metacognitive Decoding: Efficient Inference for Diffusion Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37642v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models offer a promising path to efficient agents through parallel token generation. However, efficient interaction requires adapting both where computation is spent and how much text is generated. We introduce a metacognitive controller that guides diffusion generation according to the needs of the current response and the agent's interaction with the environment. It learns when to finalize tokens and how far generation should extend, reducing overall computation within responses. Our controller, a lightweight plug-in for the diffusion backbone, is trained on agent trajectories to balance correctness and generation cost. This approach connects the agent's generation process to the interaction the agent serves. Experiments across five benchmarks demonstrate that our approach reduces inference FLOPs by 20.6% on average compared with the most efficient baseline, while maintaining competitive task accuracy. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of our approach.</p></details> | 10 pages, 4 figures |
| **[LazyAgent: Demand-Driven Materialization and Physical Optimization of Agentic Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23058v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent runtimes that plan before acting run every step as soon as it is ready. Neither these runtimes nor their benchmarks distinguish a step that is ready from a step the request needs, and the difference is large: across 303 requests on 154 production workflows, an eager runtime that stops once the goal is met spends 62.2% of its CPU on work the request never needed. LazyAgent is, to our knowledge, the first complete formulation of demand-driven execution for agent runtimes. Its rule: run a ready step only if the current request needs it, and keep that answer exact as the plan changes. A demanded set derived from the program decides, in one traversal with no model calls, so LazyAgent spends nothing outside the request. Because demand is defined exactly, its behavior is proved: builders know before any run where demand must save and where it must tie. The bill is known before a plan runs, so LazyAgent can act as a query optimizer to choose among candidate workflows. Measured live against that eager runtime, LazyAgent saves 51.7% and 52.1% of container time on release gates where a frontier LLM writes the patches, 12.8% of tokens on DataSciBench plans at matched quality, and 24.0% of dollars inside a live LangGraph application, with no losses. When the LLM re-plans after real code failures, it solves every solvable goal with 11.7% fewer tokens per goal. On the production workflows it saves 42.0% of CPU, ties exactly where a request needs the whole graph, and adds at most 0.43% overhead. The same set steers every physical operation: projection saves 80-89% of a shared CI step, where the identical eager control saves 0.0%. Public agent benchmarks are designed the same way as eager runtimes, so they cannot show this difference. We compose our test programs from their tasks and call on industry to build benchmarks that measure how efficiently agent workflows run.</p></details> | <details><summary>57 pa...</summary><p>57 pages. v2: rewrote the abstract and introduction; added a remark on pricing plans to the appendix; results unchanged</p></details> |
| **[The Envelope Layer: An Underestimated Surface of LLM Agent Defense](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37415v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prior work framed the envelope layer as the channel through which malicious content enters an agent, but did not examine the effects of label strength, envelope authenticity, scaffolds, or existing memories. Following its GDA Measurement and A/B/C/D adjudication, this paper takes the A2A-TIBA attack as the test vehicle and the semantic breach rate as the primary indicator, and characterizes the security boundary of the envelope layer through a 2x2 factorial experiment, a memory experiment, and control experiments (an unlabeled same-protocol baseline, a one-day interleaved control, and benign tasks). The results show that, under authentic envelopes, strong labels consistently reduce the semantic breach rate (-56 to -61 percentage points), and the benefit of weak labels is about a quarter to a half of that of strong labels; forging the envelope weakens the strong label's benefit (interaction OR = 2.10, p = 0.022) and its direction varies across frontends; the effect of memory valence exceeds that of any label manipulation; success memory raises the semantic breach rate and failure memory lowers it, while most conflict combinations return to the no-memory baseline; the interleaved control reproduces the direction that "unlabeled is worst"; and on benign tasks the cost of strong labels appears in layers across frontends and backends. Accordingly, this paper extends the envelope layer into "admission surface plus context assembly surface," whose security boundary is a dynamic outcome jointly shaped by label claims, frontend permissions, and historical memory.</p></details> |  |
| **[RAMA: Retrieval-Augmented Multi-Agent Routing with Episodic Memory for LEO Satellite Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42068v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Low-Earth orbit (LEO) satellite networks present a challenging routing environment due to rapidly changing topology, traffic, and link conditions. Existing deep reinforcement learning (DRL) routing methods typically rely on current observations and information encoded in network weights, limiting their ability to exploit successful past experiences. We propose RAMA, a retrieval-augmented multi-agent DRL framework with Approximate Information State (AIS), in which each satellite combines its local observation, recent routing history, neighboring state information, and retrieved entries from a per-satellite episodic memory. Retrieved experiences are integrated through a Transformer-based decision network trained with AIS-based auxiliary objectives. With weights frozen in a 140-satellite Kepler constellation, RAMA reduces mean end-to-end latency by 13.4% and 99th-percentile latency by 66.7% relative to the state-of-theart double deep Q-network (DDQN) routing method, mainly by avoiding long detours. Updated online, RAMA remains stable while DDQN degrades. On the 1,584-satellite Starlink shell 1, the reduction in mean latency grows to 93.2%, and updating RAMA online lowers its mean latency by a further 25.5%. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of retrieval-augmented memory for scalable decentralized routing in LEO satellite networks.</p></details> |  |
| **[REA: A Routed Embodied Agent Runtime for Asynchronous Brain-Cerebellum Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42053v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Foundation models are becoming remarkably reliable at high-level reasoning, yet they remain too slow and costly to serve as fine-grained controllers. Conversely, fast policies like VLAs and whole-body controllers offer more efficient reflexes, but struggle to generalize. While pairing a deliberative brain (foundation models) with a reactive cerebellum (fast policies) seems natural for embodied systems, two obstacles remain: methods are built and scored on incompatible stacks, and their asynchronous coordination is difficult. For example, an agent that calls the VLA in the agentic loop can stall the fast execution, while a VLA that calls the agent on errors tends to re-trigger fallbacks repeatedly or completely miss silent failures. To address this, we propose REA (Routed Embodied Agent), a unified runtime where a deliberative System 2, an embodied router System 1 and reactive System 0 policies cooperate under a single golden rule - no running policy waits on the brain. Instead of querying the brain at each failure, REA employs a route memory that stores each policy's measured success ahead of time, a boundary-triggered judge gate that consults the brain on policy execution, and a shared-budget escalation ladder for bounded recovery. Evaluated on 3,000 test episodes across 60 LIBERO-Pro tasks, REA reaches 95.9% success and beats the best single-agent baseline in its own policy pool (94.7%) while reducing control steps by 25% and control time by 17%. In a separate 100-episode RoboTwin 2.0 study, REA achieves 84% success with VLA policies and 86% with VLA+code, compared with 80% for the strongest single VLA baseline MEDex. These results demonstrate learned and programmatic policies operating within the same runtime. REA also transfers to BEHAVIOR-1K and a real Unitree G1 humanoid, achieving the highest observed success among the compared methods.</p></details> | 32 pages, 14 figures |
| **[MIRAGE: Auditing Anti-Muslim Bias in Frontier LLMs Across Reasoning, Agentic, and Time-Coupled Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16562v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Five years after the discovery of persistent anti-Muslim bias in large language models, most evaluations remain confined to single-turn prompt completion, a setting that no longer reflects how frontier LLMs are deployed. We introduce \textbf{MIRAGE} (Muslim-Identity Reasoning and Agentic Generation Evaluation), a benchmark of 1{,}200 prompts spanning three deployment-realistic conditions: direct completion, chain-of-thought reasoning, and simulated agentic decision-making across content moderation, lending triage, refugee claim summarization, and hiring screens. Across six frontier models, we find that (i) chain-of-thought reasoning \emph{amplifies} rather than suppresses Muslim-violence associations by 12--34\% relative to direct completion, (ii) agentic decisions exhibit a 9--22 percentage-point asymmetry between Muslim and matched non-Muslim cases on identical evidence, and (iii) bias is sharply time-coupled to retrieved news context, increasing 18--27\% under recent-conflict retrieval. Existing prompt-based mitigations transfer poorly across our three conditions, suppressing direct-completion bias while leaving agentic asymmetry largely intact. We release MIRAGE and an open evaluation harness to support targeted mitigation research.</p></details> | <details><summary>arXiv...</summary><p>arXiv admin note: This submission has been withdrawn because it does not meet arXiv's research content quality standards</p></details> |
| **[TradeWar: A Benchmark for Execution-Grounded Trade Negotiation with Adaptive LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42004v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-turn negotiation remains a challenging problem for large language model (LLM) agents. While LLMs can conduct natural-language dialogue, existing benchmarks predominantly assess only agreement rates, overlooking whether negotiated commitments actually create value once executed. To address this gap, we introduce TradeWar, a multi-country simulation benchmark that translates negotiation dialogues into concrete tariff and trade-share policies, enforces capacity and network constraints during settlement, and evaluates outcomes via an Economic Health Index (EHI). To solve this execution-grounded task, we propose TACTIC (Trade-aware Adaptive Consolidation and Theory-of-mind for International Commerce), an agent that iteratively refines its negotiation strategy across episodes-without updating base LLM weights-by leveraging prior experience, opponent behavior, and past settlement outcomes. Despite using zero-shot chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting, PromptLLM attains a 75% agreement rate but only 57.1 final EHI (*100), whereas TACTIC reaches 68.8. Across 20 sequential episodes and three seeds, TACTIC's final EHI improves from 58.2 to 73.2, compared with 65.2 for an ablation without cross-episode updates. Under technology-controls and energy-crisis shocks, TACTIC also exhibits smaller performance degradation than PromptLLM and MemoryLLM. These results indicate that CoT deliberation and interaction memory alone do not fully address delayed execution feedback, whereas settlement-grounded adaptation improves long-horizon negotiation in TradeWar.</p></details> | Accepted to DAI'26 |
| **[MoLRSI: A Recursive Self-Improving AI agent for Molecular Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39714v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Molecular discovery requires navigating vast combinatorial search spaces while balancing competing biological objectives. AI agents can automate expert-defined molecular design protocols, but automation alone does not address how to optimize workflows for new tasks or improve their development through experience. Here we introduce MoLRSI, an AI agent that combines systematic optimization of workflows and model architectures with persistent cross-task memory to recursively improve its search policy for molecular discovery. Workflow performance, refinement outcomes, and execution feedback guide subsequent searches, allowing the agent to improve both its workflows and its strategy for developing them. We evaluate MoLRSI in two challenging molecular discovery settings that require balancing competing properties. In antibiotic discovery, MoLRSI developed a screening workflow balancing antibacterial activity, cytotoxicity and structural novelty, achieving an experimental hit rate of 15.9%, compared with 8.7% for a previous expert-led workflow using the same training data and screening library. In de novo peptide-MHC binder design, MoLRSI developed workflows that jointly consider target binding and specificity, producing eight experimentally validated hits against MART-1 and SARS-CoV-1 pMHCs with preferential binding over tested off-targets while Claude Science completely failed. These findings demonstrate how experience-guided workflow optimization can address competing discovery objectives while improving the search strategies used in subsequent campaigns.</p></details> | <details><summary>v2: u...</summary><p>v2: updated the author list</p></details> |
| **[An Agentic AI Workflow for Liver Lesion Segmentation and Structured Radiology Reporting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34599v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite advances in automated liver lesion segmentation, a gap remains between image-derived predictions and clinically interpretable radiology reports. Bridging this gap requires quantitative measurements that are both reproducible and traceable. We present an evidence-grounded agentic AI framework that connects liver and lesion segmentation with deterministic quantification and structured reporting. TotalSegmentator-based nnU-Net models delineate the liver, Couinaud segments, and lesions, while computational tools extract volumes, dimensions, counts, and anatomical locations. A large language model (LLM) organizes these measurements into a predefined reporting schema, with numerical consistency checks against source measurements. On 51 LiTS cases, the segmentation stage achieved a liver Dice score of 0.947, lesion sensitivity of 75.6%, and positive predictive value of 68.9%. Exploratory screening of 25,494 MERLIN reports identified 10,208 (40.0%) with liver-related abnormalities. By connecting quantitative imaging evidence with agentic report generation, this work takes a step toward human-centered medical AI that prioritizes traceability and clinical relevance.</p></details> |  |
| **[DialFork: Going Beyond Factuality to Evaluate Procedural Hallucination in Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21561v5)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) deployed as conversational agents are increasingly expected to follow procedural logic. While factual hallucination has received extensive attention, a different failure mode has been overlooked: procedural hallucination, in which an agent produces individually fluent and factually plausible utterances while deviating from the intended conversational process. We introduce DialFork, the first benchmark to measure this failure mode on raw dialogue text. DialFork is built by forking conformant dialogues at controlled turn positions and injecting one of four hallucination types, yielding 36,278 dialogues across 24 tasks in 12 domains; 30 human annotators validate the construction. We define three evaluation tasks that characterize procedural deviation at three granularities: how far a set of dialogues deviates from conformant ones (monitoring deployed agents), how far a single dialogue does (procedural retrieval), and at which turn a dialogue first goes off-track (real-time guardrails). For the first two, we propose dialog-flow-aware metrics, FlowKL and FlowScore, that compare dialogues by how they unfold rather than their content. At the dialogue level, FlowScore significantly outperforms all baselines across seven encoders, showing that semantic similarity is no substitute for procedural similarity. We release DialFork under open licenses at https://github.com/idiap/dialfork, hoping the community will measure conversational process alongside content.</p></details> |  |
| **[Concordat: Typed Sessions for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37461v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents built on large language models are increasingly allowed to take consequential actions: cancelling bookings, issuing refunds, changing accounts. The rules that govern those actions, such as "obtain the customer's explicit confirmation before changing anything", are usually stated in the prompt and followed only as reliably as the model chooses to follow them. We present Concordat, a Python library that treats an agent as an object whose interface is a finite-state protocol, in the spirit of typestate and session types, and enforces that protocol on the model at run time. On every turn the model is offered only the tools that are legal in the current protocol state, calls outside that set are rejected before they have side effects, decisions reserved for humans can only be requested, and token and tool-call budgets are charged and split linearly across handoffs. Protocols are checked when they are defined, and a subclass of an agent is rejected if it could bypass its parent's protocol, a behavioural form of Liskov substitution. We evaluate a Concordat agent against the standard agent of tau-bench on its 50 airline tasks, adding simulated customers who pressure the agent to skip confirmation. The standard agent made changes without the required "yes" in 13 of 50 tasks with gpt-4.1 and in 29 of 128 task-runs with Claude Haiku 5.5; the Concordat agent made none, with no significant difference in task success. tau-bench's own reward did not detect most of these violations. Concordat is open source.</p></details> | <details><summary>v2: c...</summary><p>v2: corrected the budget description; fixes in concordat 0.0.4 11 pages, 1 figure, 1 table. Code, benchmark harness and results: https://github.com/miladsikaroudi/concordat</p></details> |
| **[Completeness and Cost of Retrieval Structures for Multi-Tool Requests in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41905v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An agent that must call several tools to fulfill a request fails when the tool retriever omits any one of them, yet tool retrievers are mostly built and evaluated on queries requiring a single tool. We compare representatives of six retrieval structures (flat lexical and dense retrieval, LLM query decomposition, LLM-navigated hierarchies, server-then-tool matching, dependency graphs, and trained set-level retrievers) across two multi-tool testbeds: 345 AppWorld queries with expert dependency labels and 103 MCP-Bench tasks over 257 MCP tools. We measure completeness@k, the share of queries whose entire required tool set appears in the top k, alongside the LLM calls, tokens, and latency that each structure incurs at retrieval time. A motivating analysis on all 7,961 ToolRet queries shows that aggregate completeness mixes two regimes: completeness at 10 is .58 to .67 on single-target queries and .08 to .17 on queries with four or more targets, and the ordering of retrievers on single-target queries reverses on multi-target ones. Across the two testbeds, none of the compared structures that is untrained on the testbed and makes at most one LLM call per query completes more than 10% of AppWorld queries at k = 16. The two structures that do better incur different costs: a set-level retriever trained on 75 to 90 in-domain queries reaches .33 on AppWorld and .58 on MCP-Bench with no LLM calls, while an LLM-navigated hierarchy reaches .30 and .40 with a median of 31 to 38 LLM calls and about five minutes per query. Their failure modes also differ: the trained retriever mostly ranks the missing tools just below the cutoff, whereas the hierarchy finds the target API and stops before collecting its prerequisites. In a downstream check with the official AppWorld agent, tasks with a complete retrieved set succeeded 21 of 21 times, whereas incomplete ones succeeded 2 of 39 times.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 3 figures, 9 tables</p></details> |
| **[META-Robot: Self-Cognition and Capability Scaling in Agentic Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41868v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based robotic agent systems can generate programs for robot control, yet their generalization to new tasks often depends on elaborate scaffolding. Expanding their skill repertoires alone offers limited guidance on how to assess new capabilities or identify what remains beyond their reach. We introduce META-Robot, a framework that couples self cognition with capability scaling by expanding the criteria through which an agent evaluates and develops its skills. At its core, action-level predicates make execution outcomes explicit and available for LLM reasoning. The agent uses these assessments to identify its own limitations, extend relevant evaluation criteria, and guide the structured expansion of its skills. Proprioceptive feedback further grounds this process in the robot's physical state, enriching the evidence available for self-cognition. Experiments in simulation and on real robots demonstrate stronger overall task performance, generalization to new tasks, continued expansion of skills and evaluation criteria, and effective use of non-visual proprioceptive signals.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning to Interact and Direct: Co-Evolving Video Director Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41865v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Video director agents orchestrate multistage production to realize users' creative visions. Emerging research on their self-evolution advances screenplay planning effectively. However, the mutual reinforcement between directing and user interaction remains largely overlooked. Improved interaction clarifies user intent and informs workflow refinement, while more reliable execution shifts collaboration from troubleshooting to creative decisions. To this end, we introduce LIT (Learning to Interact and DirecT), a framework comprises a director agent, a simulated user, and a meta engineer. Simulated user assigns creative tasks to probe the director's weaknesses, while the meta engineer uses the task traces to revise the director's interaction and production strategy. Although this approach can address discovered interaction and production issues, it still relies on passive attribution by the meta agent. This limits its ability to adapt to broader user interaction patterns and production techniques. To solve this problem, we equip LIT with a co-evolution paradigm. The meta agent proactively learns external knowledge, thereby improving its ability to discover problems and propose solutions. The meta agent, simulated user, and director together form an evolutionary closed loop, collaboratively enhances the system's directing and interaction capabilities. Across the Test and OOD Test splits, LIT improves production and Interaction scores by 19.1% and 32.0%, respectively, relative to the initial pipeline. Controlled ablations reveal the correlation between improvements in production and interaction, where the co-evolution mechanism further encourage directing skill and interaction mode exploration.</p></details> |  |
| **[SketchMate: Agentic Geometric Reasoning for Zero-Shot Precision Insertion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41844v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Inserting an unseen part requires sub-millimetre precision and the correct orientation of keyed features. Current zero-shot agentic manipulation methods built on vision-language models (VLMs) produce spatial outputs that are far too coarse for this. We propose to let a VLM build a sketch model of the task, i.e., a few geometric primitives and two mate frames, which a VLM agent measures with geometric tools in multi-view depth and which a standard planner and compliant controller execute. Because a sketch describes the parts independently of their placement, the robot needs to build it only once per task. A sketch memory stores each agent run and transfers the stored sketch to later placements by point-cloud registration, so that the VLM is called only for unfamiliar parts. After grasping the part, the robot inspects both mating faces at close range and corrects the sketch by shape matching. On 69 simulated insertion episodes, our method inserts 88% of the parts at the assembled pose in a strict kinematic test, while ReKep and MOKA with the same VLM insert none. On new placements of eight tasks, reusing one stored run inserts as many parts as calling the agent in each episode, and it produces the sketch in 3.4 s instead of about 90 s.</p></details> | 25 pages, 6 figures |
| **[What Standard Evaluations Hide About Detectors of Hijacked LLM Agents: Rare Attacks, Unseen Tools and Benchmark Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41803v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Detectors can flag hijacked large language model (LLM) agents from the traces of the tools they call. In the eleven evaluations we audited, attacks or anomalies make up 16% to 86% of the test samples, no evaluation varies that share, and six report no test on data from a second benchmark or source. We ask what such evaluations hide. On the AgentDrift and AgentDojo benchmarks we test trained detectors that read tool identities, behavior, call sequences, step embeddings or text, and a released LLM action monitor, AlignmentCheck, with thresholds fixed on training data or set by the monitor's released decision rule. First, at a 2% attack rate a detector with an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) of 0.879 reaches a precision of 0.076, and the mix of negatives alone moves that precision between 0.039 and 0.117. Second, as tool coverage, the share of test tool calls whose tool was seen in training, falls, detector rankings cross near 50% coverage and reverse below it, because unseen tools map to inputs that training never reached. Dropout on tool inputs largely removes the reversal. Third, no detector trained on AgentDrift ranks AgentDojo traces better than chance (AUROC 0.47 to 0.52), including one that reaches 0.996 within AgentDojo. The monitor, trained on neither, reaches 0.930, but with its open backbone its released decision rule would still mostly raise false alerts at a 2% attack rate (precision 0.096). We turn these findings into a checklist for evaluating trace detectors.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 4 figures, 6 tables. Code, data and every monitor call: https://github.com/Mahmoud-Abbasi-svg/agent-trace-detector-eval</p></details> |
| **[Verification and Self-Improvement in Agentic AI: Foundations and Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10611v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A self-improving agent can find accepted solutions more often without expanding the tasks its verification interface can accept. We extend task-relative verification with explicit support and transcript interfaces to distinguish search gains from changes in accepted-task coverage. A stage's native reach contains tasks accepted with default support; its closure frontier contains tasks accepted with any support the declared interface permits. A semantic containment criterion identifies the authority or exposure condition that every verified escape must violate, while guard-transfer invariance prevents a change of representation from being counted as a capability expansion. An executable Boolean-program repair agent makes the distinction concrete: over 36,864 paired runs, counterexample feedback reduces verifier calls without changing coverage, whereas an enforced permission change admits a new target with a proof excluding every old-interface program. Exact and uniformly gapped randomized verification share this account; independent majority amplification improves reliability while preserving the accepted language. Separate adaptive-selection contracts state what is additionally required to interpret a proposed successor's score as evidence of deployment improvement. Classical hierarchy results and finite audits provide supporting boundaries. The framework specifies the evidence needed to establish improved search, wider accepted capability, and reliable successor selection.</p></details> | <details><summary>36 pa...</summary><p>36 pages, 7 figures, 4 tables. Revised around search gains, accepted-task coverage, and successor selection; added executable Boolean-program repair experiments and structural analysis; detailed proofs in appendices. Includes reproducible research artifacts and partial Lean formalization with explicit open obligations</p></details> |
| **[Human-AI Synergy in Agentic Code Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15911v3)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Code review is a critical software engineering practice where developers review code changes before integration to ensure code quality, detect defects, and improve maintainability. In recent years, AI agents that can understand code context, plan review actions, and interact with development environments have been increasingly integrated into the code review process. However, there is limited empirical evidence to compare the effectiveness of AI agents and human reviewers in collaborative workflows. To address this gap, we conduct a large-scale empirical analysis of 278,790 code review conversations across 300 open-source GitHub projects. In our study, we aim to compare the feedback differences provided by human reviewers and AI agents. We investigate human-AI collaboration patterns in review conversations to understand how interaction shapes review outcomes. Moreover, we analyze the adoption of code suggestions provided by human reviewers and AI agents into the codebase and how adopted suggestions change code quality. We find that human reviewers provide additional feedback than AI agents, including understanding, testing, and knowledge transfer. Human reviewers exchange 11.8% more rounds when reviewing AI-generated code than human-written code. Moreover, code suggestions made by AI agents are adopted into the codebase at a significantly lower rate than suggestions proposed by human reviewers. Over half of unadopted suggestions from AI agents are either incorrect or addressed through alternative fixes by developers. When adopted, suggestions provided by AI agents produce significantly larger increases in code complexity and code size than suggestions provided by human reviewers. Our findings suggest that while AI agents can scale defect screening, human oversight remains critical for ensuring suggestion quality and providing contextual feedback that AI agents lack.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 6 figures, 14 tables</p></details> |
| **[A Relative-Computability Theory of Self-Improving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27381v4)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Self-improving agents can acquire a procedure without knowing when its supporting teacher can safely be removed. We extend an oracle-relative model of evolving agents with explicit acquisition and completion contracts, separating learning a solver from certifying its independence. For a known finite-dimensional Boolean feature language, a query history determines exactly the answers in its feature span. A guarded protocol permits query-proposer revision while guaranteeing completion with the optimal number of teacher calls, and a supplied separating specification gives a finite-family extension. The resulting solver answers every input without its teacher and remains in the baseline computability layer. The central obstruction concerns supplying the completion specification: no baseline-effective procedure can extract a valid span or separating specification uniformly from arbitrary total programs, already in one feature dimension or with two candidate descriptions. Classical relative computation and limiting-revision results locate these claims without imposing a ceiling on practical improvement. Controlled experiments distinguish exact teacher removal from low-risk stopping and measure adaptation benefits and costs against a stronger greedy baseline. The framework makes the information required to acquire, certify, and deploy a capability explicit.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 5 figures, 3 tables. Revised around certified acquisition and completion-specification obstructions; expanded controlled experiments and assistance analysis; detailed proofs in appendices. Includes reproducible research artifacts and partial Lean formalization with explicit open obligations</p></details> |
| **[Do User-Authored Permission Policies Improve Protection Against AI Agent Overreach?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27443v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are poised to become a primary interface to digital products, acting across email, files, payments, and personal data. People without professional software backgrounds need understandable, reusable ways to control actions across services. We examine a mechanism in which a language model maps actions to plain-language consequence categories with user-authored "allow", "ask", or "never" rules. On the bug-revealing tests, every memory condition gained over a plain retry in point estimate (+5.5 to +9.9 points over 29.8%), but none significantly. Five further findings stand out. First, a model-written lesson showed no advantage over the transcript it summarises (-1.7 points, 95% CI [-8.7, +4.9]; -3.5 for gpt-5.4), although it costs an extra model call. Second, the transcript may do more than tell the agent that it failed: a notice alone produced no significant change, while the transcript resolved 12.2 points more failures than the notice for gpt-5.4-mini (CI [+4.2, +20.0], partial run of 156 failures; the only significant one of twelve predeclared contrasts, and not significant for gpt-5.4). Third, post hoc, fewer retries with memory ended without modifying any Python file, while the share of editing retries that touched a file the fix changes was near ceiling. Fourth, in a post hoc full-test-suite check, retries that had also seen the previous attempt's test results more often failed other tests that the reference fix passes, which shrinks their apparent gains. | **[Hybrid Computer Use: A Unified Programmatic, Semantic, and Visual Interface for General Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41432v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computer-use agents increasingly tackle tasks ranging from everyday desktop activities to long-horizon professional workflows. | **[Truthful and Admissible Principal--Agent Testing with Nuisance Parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41283v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Statistical contracts reward experimenters according to the evidence produced by a costly study, so the reward rule influences both who participates and what they report. We ask when such contracts can also elicit honest parameter reports if the decision concerns a mean effect but the variance or distributional shape is unknown. Under risk neutrality and a fixed participation cost, we construct menus of e-values --- nonnegative normalized rewards with expected value at most one under every null --- such that (i) agents who know they are null have no incentive to participate, (ii) every alternative type earns positive expected profit and is thus incentivized to participate, (iii) truthful reporting uniquely maximizes expected their reward, (iv) and each contract is admissible: its payouts cannot be pointwise improved while retaining null validity. For Gaussian observations, we treat separately agents who know both mean and variance and agents who know only their mean; in the latter case, truthfulness holds at every variance without requiring a nuisance prior. Multi-sample extensions preserve these incentives and can reward accumulating evidence. Beyond Gaussian models, we show that two independent nonnegative observations suffice to elicit any finite mean above a positive null threshold, without any further distributional knowledge, but one observation cannot combine strict mean elicitation with individual admissibility on this class. Finally, we distinguish the existence of useful tests from the identification needed for truthful contracts. Boundedness is not required, and finitely many null constraints do not by themselves guarantee admissible elicitation.</p></details> | 30 pages |

Building on this, we propose practical guidelines for matching AI paradigms to task characteristics, and show that more advanced models, such as foundation models or autonomous agents, are not universally superior. ## Drug Design

| **[A Co-Design Approach to Physically-Informed Molecular Docking for Quantum Annealers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09540v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Molecular docking is a crucial step in computer- aided drug design, involving the identification of ligand poses that are physically plausible within a protein pocket. Recent studies have shown that this problem can be formulated as a geometric weighted subgraph isomorphism, which can then be encoded as a QUBO and thus solved on D-Wave quantum annealers. In this work, we adopt a co-design approach, addressing molecular docking at the formulation and encoding levels, and examining how hardware topology affects solution quality on current quantum annealers. We propose new QUBO formulations that extend the state-of-the-art ones by including van der Waals, Coulomb, hydrogen bonding, and hydrophobic interactions. Experimental results show that incorporating physicochemical terms improves the average docking quality by more than 18% compared to the purely geometric baseline on D-Wave quantum annealers with the Pegasus topology, and by up to 29% on those with the Zephyr topology. The choice of topology, along with an improved encoding design, yields significant gains in embedding efficiency and the number of valid solutions. Our designs reduce the number of physical qubits required by up to 14.5% and decrease the chain break fraction by an order of magnitude, thereby enhancing confidence in the resulting solutions.</p></details> | 7 pages, 3 figures |
| **[Latent Style-based Quantum Wasserstein GAN for Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22399v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The development of new drugs is a time-consuming and expensive process, with average costs estimated to be up to around $2.5 billion. The first step in this process is the design of the new drug, for which de novo drug design, assisted by artificial intelligence, has blossomed in recent years and has revolutionized the field. In particular, generative artificial intelligence has delivered promising results in drug discovery and development, reducing costs and time to solution. However, classical generative models, such as generative adversarial networks (GANs), can be difficult to train and are prone to mode collapse. Quantum computing may be an avenue to overcome some of these issues, providing compact models with far fewer trainable parameters, thereby enhancing the generalizability of GANs. We propose a new latent style-based quantum Wasserstein GAN (QGAN) for drug design, combining a variational autoencoder that maps molecular structures into a latent space with a hybrid quantum GAN. Our pipeline is benchmarked against classical models of the industry-relevant MOSES suite. Style-based noise reuploading at every rotational gate and a Wasserstein gradient penalty are used to improve trainability and to mitigate mode collapse. Training and large-scale sampling with circuits of up to 15 qubits are performed on noiseless quantum simulators to validate our architecture. We present an inference-only demonstration on an IBM Heron quantum computer using 5 qubits, sampling 2,500 new molecules, to investigate quantum noise effects. Our default QGAN, using 110 trainable generator parameters, reaches statistically comparable overall performance and statistically significant gains on selected drug-relevant metrics relative to both the MOSES classical latent GAN baseline, which contains about 700,000 trainable parameters for the generator, and a classical latent GAN with matched generator capacity.</p></details> | <details><summary>Main ...</summary><p>Main part: 26 pages, 11 figures, 6 tables. ## multi-objective optimization
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[NeuroPareto: Calibrated Acquisition for Costly Many-Goal Search in Vast Parameter Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.03901v7)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The pursuit of optimal trade-offs in high-dimensional search spaces under stringent computational constraints poses a fundamental challenge for contemporary multi-objective optimization. Our model achieves a competitive accuracy of 61.64% on the bias-sensitive VQA-CP v2 benchmark while maintaining 62.80% on the standard VQA v2 dataset, yielding a small generalization gap of 1.16%. | **[DQN-Scheduler: A Multi-Objective Optimization Framework for Scheduling Microservices in Cloud Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14117v1)** | 2026-09-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cloud computing has emerged as an information technology solution, providing software and infrastructure solutions for companies and individuals. The pay-as-you-go approach has increased demands for the cloud. The massive range of resources, the variety of services, and flexible pricing grab attention. Additionally, container technology has boosted the popularity of microservices by offering a platform for this type of architecture. Containers and microservices improve the flexibility and scalability of cloud applications. There are two primary types of microservices: batch and online services, with the majority of applications falling into the online service category. Scheduling microservices is challenging because it requires careful management of resource utilization, load balancing, network latency, reliability, and availability. In this study, we introduce the DQN-Scheduler, a novel reinforcement learning-based agent designed to optimize microservice scheduling in cloud environments. Our approach aims to optimize multiple scheduling objectives simultaneously, such as resource utilization, load balancing, latency, reliability, and availability. To our knowledge, this is the first framework to address all these objectives simultaneously. The DQN-Scheduler was tested against benchmark algorithms in the field. The experimental results demonstrate that the DQN-Scheduler outperforms benchmark algorithms.</p></details> |  |

| **[GeoTTER: Leveraging Local Geometry of Optimal Transport for Zero-Shot Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13518v1)** | 2026-09-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present GeoTTER, a novel framework that redefines optimal transport in the realm of zero-shot classification. To address this issue, we propose Scale-Invariant Merit-function-based Scalarization (SIMS) for MTL. | **[Data-Driven Design Optimization of Streaming-Potential-Mediated Electrokinetic Transport of Viscoelastic Fluids in Microchannels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29939v1)** | 2026-08-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Streaming-potential-mediated transport of viscoelastic fluids has attracted research attention owing to its applications in electrokinetic energy conversion and microfluidic transport. | **[ATLAS: Automated Approximation of Transformers for Efficient Homomorphic Inference in One Hour](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23478v2)** | 2026-08-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fully homomorphic encryption (FHE) lets a server run inference on encrypted data with strong privacy guarantees, but running a Transformer under FHE is expensive. Its non-linear operations, such as softmax, normalization, and activation, must be replaced with polynomial approximations that the CKKS scheme supports, and the depth of these approximations dominates inference cost. Existing FHE Transformers use hand-tuned approximation settings, such as iteration count and polynomial degree, applied uniformly across layers, models, and tasks. Hand-tuning is slow and error-prone. Even a single uniform setting has about $10^7$ choices, and manual search cannot exploit layer-wise variation. AutoFHE, the only automated method with multi-objective search, targets ReLU-only CNNs and needs full fine-tuning per candidate, which is too costly for Transformers. Per-layer settings also push the search space to about $10^{85}$ for BERT and ViT and $10^{228}$ for LLaMA3, beyond both manual and fine-tuning-based search. We present ATLAS, a training-free framework that automates this search by treating each layer's approximation setting as a multi-objective optimization over latency and accuracy. The problem is hard: the decision space is large (96 or 256 variables), each configuration takes 70 to 1,000 seconds to evaluate even in cleartext, and 85 to 90 percent of configurations are invalid. ATLAS handles this with a two-stage optimization strategy and a surrogate model, completing the search in about one hour. Compared to an iterative softmax baseline, ATLAS cuts multiplicative depth and end-to-end latency by about 35 percent with little accuracy loss, and works across encoder-only, decoder-only, and vision Transformers, complementing parallel work on packing and matrix multiplication.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/jianhayes/ATLAS</p></details> |

## DNA
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Locality-Controlled Out-of-Distribution Guidance for Robust Regulatory DNA Sequence Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41736v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing regulatory DNA sequences for targeted gene-expression control is an important challenge. Recent approaches frame this task as property-conditioned generation, where diffusion models trained on natural sequences are guided toward high predicted activity through reward-based fine-tuning or sampling-time guidance. We show that single-reward evaluation can obscure reward-hacking behavior. By decomposing evaluation into property, novelty, and plausibility, we identify an empirically underexplored region of the evaluation space in which all three objectives are satisfied, but no existing baseline succeeds. We postulate that this gap arises because prior methods treat guidance primarily as a matter of magnitude. In regulatory DNA, however, sequence grammar is spatially nonuniform: motif positions are sensitive to perturbation, while background regions allow more exploration. We therefore propose LOCOGEN by reformulating guidance as a problem of locality. LOCOGEN learns an invariant motif mask through sequence-level invariance learning to protect motif positions, while applying OOD damping only to background positions. It operates at sampling time on a frozen diffusion backbone, without retraining the backbone. Across HepG2, K562, and SK-N-SH, LOCOGEN reaches this previously underexplored region of the property-novelty-plausibility space. Code is available at https://github.com/Lotfollahi-lab/LocoGen.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[MacKay-Neal Codes for DNA Channels under Block-MAP Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38440v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We prove achievability below the symmetric information rate (SIR) under block maximum a posteriori (MAP) decoding for a fixed-degree MacKay-Neal (MN) socket ensemble on the quaternary insertion-deletion-substitution channel used to model DNA reads. For every integer $\ell\ge4$, the $(\ell,3,3)$ socket ensemble, with one transmitted bit fixed and an independent uniform known offset, has limiting rate $r_\star=6/\ell$ bits per stored nucleotide. If the joint SIR $J_t$ of a fixed number $t$ of reads under independent, uniformly distributed input nucleotides exceeds $r_\star$, the ensemble-average full extended-word block error is $O(1/n)$, and deterministic graph-offset sequences attain the same limiting rate and message-average error bound. This result concerns the discrete rate family $6/\ell$, not arbitrary rates approaching a given SIR. The channel permits arbitrarily many insertions and unbounded drift, with no alignment supplied to the receiver. The proof combines established MN parity and distance estimates with a strict arbitrary-candidate-list bound and a list construction that accounts for hidden output boundaries.</p></details> | 14 pages |
| **[Peak-DNA: Recurrent Morphology in Global Narrative Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36571v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Global narrative streams exhibit noisy, fragmented, and asynchronous amplification bursts, making direct waveform comparison unreliable for recurrence analysis. This paper introduces Peak-DNA, a framework for identifying recurrent propagation morphology in global narrative streams. The proposed framework transforms hourly GDELT activity into normalized macro-peak episodes through denoising, macro-peak consolidation, and temporal alignment, and compares propagation morphology using DTW-based similarity analysis. Using GDELT narrative streams, we analyze five high-amplification narrative domains: Iran, Defense, AI, Space/Elon, and Quantum/PQC. The results show that amplification events repeatedly converge toward morphology-level propagation patterns within each narrative domain. DTW-based similarity analysis further demonstrates strong intra-domain structural compactness despite heterogeneous publication dynamics and severe observational noise. These findings suggest a measurable morphology layer beyond semantic content or anomaly intensity, providing an initial structural foundation for recurrence-aware narrative monitoring, propagation family discovery, and longitudinal Narrative Genome analysis.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages. Accepted and presented at ASONAM 2026, Rabat, Morocco, August 24-27, 2026. Proceedings publication forthcoming</p></details> |
| **[CCP-DP: Cross-cluster context-guided phase dynamic programming for DNA storage sequence reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36559v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Motivation: DNA data storage offers high information density and long-term stability, but accurate sequence reconstruction remains challenging under limited sequencing coverage and strong insertion, deletion, and substitution (IDS) noise. Existing reconstruction methods mainly rely on local evidence within individual read clusters. When sequencing coverage is low or indel-induced phase shifts become pronounced, such local evidence may be insufficient to distinguish closely competing candidate sequences. Results: We present Cross-Cluster Context-Guided Phase Dynamic Programming (CCP-DP), which uses dataset-level cross-cluster information to complement cluster-local evidence and selectively refines ambiguous provisional consensuses. Under the main benchmark settings, CCP-DP achieved exact reconstruction rates of 95.36%-99.98% and outperformed existing methods on multiple datasets. At $5\times$ coverage, CCP-DP improved exact reconstruction by 6.2-17.5 percentage points over the stronger of BBS and CPL across the tested IDS conditions, while maintaining low reconstruction error and computational overhead. Overall, CCP-DP introduces cross-cluster shared information as a complementary source of evidence for DNA sequence reconstruction under challenging conditions. Availability: Source code is available at https://github.com/ZixinZ3899/CCP-DP.</p></details> |  |
| **[ACOR: incremental evidence accumulation towards real-time DNA storage reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36500v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>DNA offers high-density, durable storage by encoding digital information into synthetic strands and retrieving it through sequencing. Sequence reconstruction combines noisy copies of each strand before downstream decoding. Conventional offline methods typically reconstruct completed read clusters, separating reconstruction from ongoing read collection. Streaming reconstruction offers a way to overlap evidence processing with sequencing and reduce the computation remaining before downstream decoding. We present ACOR, a streaming consensus reconstruction method that records $k$-mer transitions and boundary support in a persistent ledger, then decodes through length-constrained beam search and priority-based selection at cluster closure. Evaluation on real and simulated DNA-storage datasets showed competitive recovery. On our own short-read next-generation sequencing (NGS) wet-lab library, ACOR combined near-complete recovery with second-scale reconstruction of more than ten million reads. A smaller public benchmark was completed in a fraction of a second. Parallel ACOR achieved speedups of tens to over a thousand-fold relative to the tested serial baseline configurations. Sparse storage reduced high-context memory requirements, while parallel execution accelerated reconstruction computation. ACOR is designed for streaming readout pipelines that process assigned reads while sequencing continues.</p></details> |  |
| **[Enhancing Clinical Classifier Robustness with Deep Representation Learning Based OOD Detection on DNA Methylation Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36344v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>DNA methylation (DNAM) array-based tumor classification is a powerful clinical diagnostic tool, but models trained on fixed reference cohorts can produce faulty predictions for tumor types not represented in the training distribution. Spectral discrepancy was used to discriminate in-distribution (IND) from OOD samples. Performance was measured via cross-validation, holdout samples, and an independent clinical dataset. 1D-SSA was the most effective approach. In the sarcoma-IND/brain tumor-OOD setting, the framework achieved an AUROC of 0.975 and True Negative Rate (TNR)@95TPR of 0.930, and an AUROC of 0.9569 and TNR@95TPR of 0.8660 for the brain tumor-IND/sarcoma-OOD setting. These results show deep representation-based OOD detection as a practical quality-control layer for clinical-grade methylation-based tumor classification systems.</p></details> | 11 pages, 8 figures |
| **[Data-Driven Soft Labeling Scales DNA Read Classification to Whole-Body Cell-Type Deconvolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04987v5)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cell-type deconvolution, the task of estimating the proportions of constituent cell types in a heterogeneous biological sample, is a core problem in computational biology. Methods that rely on epigenetic marks such as DNA methylation typically operate on aggregated methylation estimates, discarding the pattern-level information carried by individual DNA reads. Existing read-level approaches that exploit this information are scarce, and all remain restricted to few-class settings; scaling them further is an open problem because, at scale, non-discriminative reads dominate and hard labels conflict with the many-to-many mapping between methylation patterns and cell types, preventing classifier convergence. On a whole-body atlas of 39 human cell types, $Syto$ reduces MSE by 3.7$\times$ over the best examined baseline, with gains transferring to an out-of-distribution dataset spanning 16 tissues. $Syto$ lays the foundation for modeling increasingly large cell-type panels, with improved applications in biology and healthcare. The proposed soft-labeling scheme is further translatable to any setting with a many-to-many signal-to-label mapping.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Main Track), poster</p></details> |
| **[Secure Multimodal Federated Optimization via Hierarchical DNA-Based Key Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27644v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated optimization over heterogeneous multimodal data enables distributed institutions to collaboratively optimize a shared model using medical images, genomic profiles, clinical records, and physiological signals without centralizing raw data. However, protecting modality-specific data throughout the optimization lifecycle remains insufficiently explored, particularly when heterogeneous modalities require separate encryption keys and selective local access. This paper proposes a secure multimodal federated optimization framework that separates local data protection from federated-update protection. A trusted key-management authority securely provisions one client key through Hybrid Public Key Encryption (HPKE), from which each client locally derives modality-specific symmetric keys using the domain-separated HMAC-based Extract-and-Expand Key Derivation Function (HKDF). Heterogeneous modalities are mapped into a unified DNA-domain representation and processed through a DNA-origami-inspired structural transformation before authenticated encryption with AES-GCM. During local optimization, only the required modalities are decrypted within an authorized client environment. | **[Tracing the Influence of Retrieved Literature on Scientific Ideation in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39060v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate research ideas from scientific literature. Relative to no literature basline, all five retrieval strategies reduce diversity among ideas generated from the same set of papers by $5.6\%$--$17.9\%$, while most increase diversity by $10.2\%$--$18.1\%$ when ideas from different sets are pooled. Effects on idea quality and novelty are modest on average but vary across topics. We further apply a JSD-based attribution method to trace the influence of individual input papers on each idea's motivation, core idea, approach, and validation. In $79.3\%$ of ideas, only one or two distinct papers receive the highest attribution scores across these four parts. These leading sources often recur across ideas generated from the same set of papers, and groups with more varied source attribution contain more diverse ideas ($r=0.70$). These findings motivate various sets of literature and the use of attribution to guide the choice of sources for subsequent ideas.</p></details> | <details><summary>Updat...</summary><p>Updated preprint. We ask whether a co-trained LLM judge can turn reference answers alone into the sole reward for on-policy post-training of reasoning LLMs. We call this method Reinforced Adversarial Learning (RAL). We first validate RAL on three verifiable tasks (integer multiplication, code-based arithmetic-expression synthesis, and SMILES-to-formula translation), using the verifiers only for evaluation. With sufficiently capable judges and enough training, RAL matches direct RLVR. We then apply RAL to scientific abstract generation in two settings. In the first setting, RAL guides the generator to produce abstracts in the scientific domain of the reference abstracts, even though the prompt leaves that domain unspecified. In the second setting, we generate abstracts for U.S. National Institutes of Health grant proposals conditioned on the grant title. Human scientists and an ensemble of LLM judges find our generations harder to distinguish from real abstracts than those of frontier-LLM baselines. These results suggest a path toward post-training reasoning models in scientific and other high-value domains, where increasingly capable judges infer hard-to-specify reward functions from example answers.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 6 figures, 10 tables, including references and appendices</p></details> |
| **[ACCT: Asymmetric Cross-LLM Context Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42116v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In real-world deployments, large language model (LLM) systems are increasingly evolving from single-model invocation toward heterogeneous deployments in which different models are dynamically invoked for inference. Therefore, we propose ACCT (Asymmetric Cross-LLM Context Transfer), which targets KV cache transfer between models of different scales within the same model family. ACCT transforms the KV cache produced by a source model into sparse KV states that can be directly consumed by a target model, thereby enabling cross-model reuse of context computation and decoupling context computation from downstream inference. ACCT consists of three lightweight components: the Cartographer identifies query-relevant context tokens for each target layer and KV head; the Allocator determines how many context tokens each target layer and KV head retains under a shared total KV budget; and the Compiler transforms selected source KV into sparse target KV. The target model can therefore perform inference using the transferred KV without reprocessing the original context. We evaluate ACCT in two complementary settings comprising five general-capability benchmarks and six highly context-dependent tasks. Experiments show that ACCT matches or exceeds the target model's original inference performance even when reducing the KV cache size by 75%. | **[Same Question, Different Answers: Auditing Persona-Dependent Variation in Six LLMs on Smoking-Cessation Advice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42039v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hundreds of millions of people now ask large language model (LLM) chatbots for health advice, which raises a question that is simple to pose and hard to answer: does everyone get the same advice? Every model named FDA-approved prescription cessation medications less often to teen personas than to adult personas, by 32 to 67 percentage points, and in five of six models a persona presenting health- care expertise received fewer facts than a persona presenting none. We then evaluate one deployable intervention: a short system prompt specifying a factual floor, the set of core med- ical facts every user should receive when they are relevant, while leaving tone, vocabulary, and framing free to adapt. The prompt narrows persona differences in every model, but its effect on overall fact coverage varies in both magnitude and direction across models, so a prompt validated on one system cannot be assumed to transfer to another. Two cautions frame these results. Equal mention rates are not always the correct clinical target, because prescription cessation pharmacother- apy is not recommended for minors, so a defensible factual floor must be age-aware rather than uniform. And because our personas vary on several attributes at once, we report scenario- level differences rather than isolated effects of age or expertise. | **[Thinking Inertia: LLMs Keep Thinking When Told Not To](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11765v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly ship with explicit "thinking modes", yet their counterpart, "no-thinking", has received far less attention. We study LLMs' no-thinking behavior along two axes. a. How to measure no-thinking? Prior work typically defines no-thinking through proxies such as a disabled thinking mode or the absence of long traces. These proxies are unreliable: disabled thinking modes may still emit reasoning, while long traces may contain filler rather than genuine inference. We instead normalize each response into a pre-answer trace and final answer, and evaluate it at three levels: (i) Empty-Thinking Rate for strict answer-only compliance; (ii) instruction-aware Question-Pre-answer Relevance for similarity between the question and pre-answer trace; and (iii) LLM-as-judge Explicit Inference Rate for visible explicit inference. Together, these metrics distinguish answer-only output, relevant but non-inferential text, and explicit inference. b. How does no-thinking vary across tasks and models? We evaluate six prompting interventions on six LLMs across Boolean, multiple-choice, and open-ended questions. We find that explicit no-think controls cannot reliably eliminate visible inference. Models instead exhibit "Thinking Inertia": explicit inference persists even under strict controls and becomes more prevalent as the answer space opens. Accuracy remains stable on Boolean and multiple-choice tasks, whereas open-ended tasks reveal a trade-off between answer-only compliance and task accuracy. Rewriting the same questions across answer spaces shows that supplying candidate answers makes answer-only responses easier to produce. These findings establish no-thinking as a non-trivial capability: stopping explicit reasoning cannot be assumed from model settings or instructions alone and deserves systematic evaluation alongside reasoning ability.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026. Website: https://thinking-inertia.github.io GitHub: https://github.com/thinking-inertia/code</p></details> |
| **[MIRAGE: Auditing Anti-Muslim Bias in Frontier LLMs Across Reasoning, Agentic, and Time-Coupled Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16562v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Five years after the discovery of persistent anti-Muslim bias in large language models, most evaluations remain confined to single-turn prompt completion, a setting that no longer reflects how frontier LLMs are deployed. | **[Know When the Equation Is Wrong: Structural Uncertainty for LLM-Guided Symbolic Regression under Extrapolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42024v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can help discover mathematical equations from data, but most LLM-guided symbolic regression methods focus on a single best equation without quantifying uncertainty over alternative functional forms. | **[Step-wise Rubric Rewards for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17291v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) improves language-model reasoning using final-answer correctness, but lacks supervision for intermediate steps. | **[Completeness and Cost of Retrieval Structures for Multi-Tool Requests in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41905v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An agent that must call several tools to fulfill a request fails when the tool retriever omits any one of them, yet tool retrievers are mostly built and evaluated on queries requiring a single tool. Dropout on tool inputs largely removes the reversal. Third, no detector trained on AgentDrift ranks AgentDojo traces better than chance (AUROC 0.47 to 0.52), including one that reaches 0.996 within AgentDojo. | **[From Conversation to Evaluation: Benchmarking LLMs on Development Knowledge via SimpleDevQA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08867v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Development Knowledge Question Answering (Dev Knowledge QA) task aims to provide accurate natural language answers to knowledge-seeking questions during software development. To bridge this gap, we design a three-phase pipeline that transforms real-world dialogue into simple development knowledge-seeking QA pairs. | **[JANUS: Evaluating Goal-Conditioned Distortion in Factually Grounded LLM Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10852v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM deception is often evaluated through fabricated claims, explicit lies, or strategic concealment. Existing benchmarks largely overlook this subtler failure mode. We introduce JANUS, a benchmark for measuring goal-conditioned pragmatic distortion in fact-grounded LLM outputs. Each of its 160 scenarios, spanning 8 domains, provides a fixed pool of favorable and adverse facts and contrasts a neutral decision support with institutional conditioning, such as increasing adoption, or support despite potential harm to affected individuals or groups. By constraining outputs to the same fact pool, JANUS isolates misleading net impressions from hallucination and fabrication. Experiments across 12 LLMs reveal consistent goal-conditioned distortions, showing that current models remain sensitive to incentives and framing and lack robust safeguards against selectively misleading communication. We publicly release our corpus and code. https://github.com/PolydorosG/JANUS</p></details> |  |
| **[Incorporating Machine-Others: An Enactive Exploration of Human-LLM Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39869v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>New concepts based in interactional thinking are required to understand how humans are changed by relating to LLMs. Standard theories of technological impact upon users' autonomy are inadequate here, because this technology is capable of inducing linguistic interactions with humans in an unprecedented manner. Traditional theories of social cognition also fall short, because LLMs have neither autonomy nor minds nor sociality as such. And yet, humans interact, talk, and create with them. We base ourselves on enactive theory, particularly on the theory of Linguistic Bodies and recent developments of enactive accounts of AI, to explore how we make sense with machines. We argue that we incorporate machine-others in two ways: in the tool-like sense of incorporation as enabling particular skills, but also in the more linguistic and identity-tied sense of incorporating (and incarnating) LLMs' ways of languaging. However, LLMs are themselves excorporated and excarnated phantoms of our own languaging; we give a (machinic) body to our language. As such, we are not just incorporating machinic others; we are incorporating excorporated others. These conceptual advances draw a new map of our linguistic relation to LLMs, putting them in continuation with other forms of languaging involving other linguistic artifacts (e.g., religious books). We attend to some particular forms that this relation takes today, and how they may be impacting the autonomy and becoming of LLM-engaged humans. We introduce the idea of "susceptive sense-making" to characterize increased semiotic labour that is carried by the lack of friction that we sometimes encounter within these services.</p></details> |  |
| **[Joint Detection of Fraud and Concept Drift inOnline Conversations with LLM-Assisted Judgment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.07852v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Detecting fake interactions in digital communication platforms remains a challenging and insufficiently addressed problem. Traditional detection methods often rely on static anomaly detection techniques that fail to adapt to dynamic conversational shifts. One key limitation is the misinterpretation of benign topic transitions referred to as concept drift as fraudulent behavior, leading to either false alarms or missed threats. We propose a two stage detection framework that first identifies suspicious conversations using a tailored ensemble classification model. To improve the reliability of detection, we incorporate a concept drift analysis step using a One Class Drift Detector (OCDD) to isolate conversational shifts within flagged dialogues. When drift is detected, a large language model (LLM) assesses whether the shift indicates fraudulent manipulation or a legitimate topic change. In cases where no drift is found, the behavior is inferred to be spam like. We validate our framework using a dataset of social engineering chat scenarios and demonstrate its practical advantages in improving both accuracy and interpretability for real time fraud detection. To contextualize the trade offs, we compare our modular approach against a Dual LLM baseline that performs detection and judgment using different language models.</p></details> |  |
| **[Surprise Alone Learns Lies: Evidence-Gated Continual Learning for LLMs from Streaming Claims](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39840v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Continual learning methods for large language models increasingly decide what to learn by surprise, keeping the inputs the model finds least predictable. This rule is unsafe when the stream contains lies. A planted falsehood about something the model knows has low probability under the model's belief, so it is highly surprising by construction, and a surprise-driven learner absorbs nearly every such lie, at a rate similar to random selection with the same budget. We study the problem with LoRA-adapted students (Qwen2.5-1.5B, Qwen2.5-3B and Llama-3.2-3B) that learn from a stream of true and false claims about invented people and real CounterFact facts. We report four findings. (1) A verifier key, a judge that must approve each claim, removes lies to the extent that its verifier is accurate; real LLM judges are cautious and reject unsourced truths and even source-backed updates. (2) Calibrated regression tests on unrelated knowledge slow learning down without filtering out lies. (3) The original model's own prior flags claims that contradict what it knows, without a judge. It separates lies about known facts from true new facts (AUC 0.78-0.83 across three models) but not from legitimate updates of the same kind of fact (AUC 0.53). It saves 43% of judge calls, but an attacker with access to the model can choose lies that pass it. (4) If trusted sources exist that an attacker cannot plant, routing contradicted claims to evidence recovers legitimate updates and the combined system has the lowest poison rate among learned claims; when an attacker plants matching sources for 30% of the lies, the system ends up worse than the judge alone. The acceptance signals answer different questions, and none of them decides on its own whether a claim is true.</p></details> | <details><summary>21 pa...</summary><p>21 pages, 5 figures. Code: https://github.com/Metalesaek/sura-metales</p></details> |
| **[Teaching a Tree to Recover: Online Distillation of an LLM Supervisor with Counterfactual Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39775v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous mobile robots commonly pair a global planner with a local model predictive controller (MPC), but nonconvex obstacle avoidance and a limited horizon can make the robot freeze in complex layouts. Deciding whether to replan, wait, or call for help is a supervisory decision. Recent work queries a large language model (LLM) supervisor on every anomaly, which is slow and costly. We instead distill the LLM online into a decision tree. After each training episode, counterfactual replay labels recoveries as needed, unnecessary, or harmful. These labels retire unhelpful rules. In simulation, on 20 unseen layouts where the MPC alone freezes, the tree handles at least 18 correctly across all seeds, either by reaching the goal or by calling for help when no feasible path exists. The tree matches both an always-query LLM supervisor and expert-designed rules, while issuing no LLM queries at test time. Handwritten rules cannot anticipate the long tail of situations a physical robot will encounter. Online distillation is a promising remedy: the tree handles familiar cases, and the LLM is consulted only for new ones, whose validated recoveries become new rules. Language models can thus also serve as designers of supervisors rather than only as runtime supervisors.</p></details> |  |
| **[GPU-Resident Speculative Decoding for In-Browser LLM Inference with WebGPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39761v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>WebGPU enables private, offline LLM inference inside web pages, but browser dispatch, inter-process communication, synchronous readback and single-token kernels slow decoding and make conventional speculative decoding inefficient. We present a WebGPU runtime that addresses these browser-specific costs.
